It started out simple, you know, just a few songs and maybe some pictures, but then I got to thinking about the old places we used to go, and who should be invited, and the displays, and now I’m on the phone most days tracking people down and planning trips and all that.\n\nMy wife says I’ve let the garden go, and I haven’t been showing up for the walking group much either. She says I’ve pushed everything else aside for it. I don’t know, it just feels important, but it’s gotten bigger than I meant it to.\n\nDoctor: While you’ve been working on the memorial, have you felt unusually upbeat or energized compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose I have. Some days I feel sort of lit up, like I’ve got this purpose, like if I get it all right then the whole town will finally understand what our family was about.\n\nBut then if my wife says I’m overdoing it, or someone at the senior center starts questioning where the display should go, I get sharp with them. I don’t mean to, but I snap, like they’re getting in the way of something important.\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “need” less sleep. I mean, some nights I’m up later because I start thinking about who still needs calling, or I remember a box of photos in the closet—funny, my brother had this old fishing picture everyone used to laugh about—and then I’m looking for it and the evening gets away from me.\n\nBut whether I’m actually sleeping less than usual… I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that you’ve been talking faster than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, my pastor has said something like that. At the senior center meetings, I start explaining how the songs fit with the quilts and the photos, and then one thing reminds me of another—like the hymn my brother liked, though honestly he’d only admit it after a couple coffees—and before I know it, I’ve talked right over three people.\n\nMy pastor will sort of put his hand up gentle and say, “Aaron, let’s let someone else speak.” So… yes, I guess people have noticed I’m going on too fast or too much.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt that you have a special ability or unique responsibility to make this memorial matter more than anyone else could?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it like that… “special ability” sounds awfully grand. I just—well, I’ve been around long enough to remember how my mother set tables for half the neighborhood, and how my brother used to fix people’s porch steps without taking a dime, and those things get lost if somebody doesn’t put them in the right order.\n\nNow, whether that means I’m the only one who can do it… I don’t know. My wife says other people can help, and she’s probably right. It just feels like I know the pieces, you know? Like that picture from the church picnic—half the folks in it are gone now, and if I don’t say who they are, then who’s going to?\n\nDoctor: Have you been more active than usual in planning or doing things lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. That’s kind of what brought me in, really. This memorial for my brother started as a little thing, you know, maybe a few songs and some pictures, and now I’m spending most days chasing down hymns, calling people, figuring out displays, even thinking about trips to pick up things or talk to folks.\n\nAnd I mean, it all feels important—my brother was the kind of man who’d show up with a toolbox before you even asked—but my wife says I’ve let the garden go, and I haven’t been to the walking group much, and just about everything else has gotten pushed aside. So yes, I’ve been doing a lot more than usual, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this unusually energized and driven?\n\nPatient: About two months, I’d say. It really started after my brother passed and I began working on his tribute. At first it was just, you know, picking a song or two, maybe sorting some photos, but then I kept seeing more that ought to be included—people he helped, stories from church, little things like that.\n\nSo… yes, roughly two months I’ve been feeling this driven about it.\n\nDoctor: Before your brother died, had you ever had another period when you felt unusually energized and driven like this?\n\nPatient: Well… before he died? I’m trying to think. I’ve always been a person who gets involved in things—church suppers, fixing up the house, helping my wife with odds and ends—but this particular push, this kind of being pulled along by it, has really been these last couple months.\n\nIt started after my brother passed and I began putting together the tribute for him. Before that, I don’t know that I’d say it was like this. This has been going on about two months, tied up with the memorial.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you felt more important or more capable than usual?\n\nPatient: More important? I don’t know… I haven’t really thought of it that way. I mean, I do feel like this memorial needs to be done right, but that’s because he was my brother, you know? He helped a lot of people.\n\nCapable… well, I’ve been making calls and arranging things, but I wouldn’t say I suddenly feel like some big important man. Though my wife did say I was acting like I was running the whole church committee, and that reminds me, Mrs. Hanley used to run those bake sales like a general—she could get forty pies donated by Thursday without raising her voice.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I feel responsible for it, I guess, more than important.\n\nDoctor: Aside from working on your brother’s memorial, have you been taking on other new projects or activities more than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, not so much new projects besides the memorial, I guess. It’s more that the memorial itself keeps turning into ten different projects. I start with one song, then I’m calling someone about photos, then I’m planning a display, then I think maybe I should drive over and talk to somebody who knew him from church years back.\n\nMost days I’m wrapped up in that—songs, calls, displays, little trips—and my wife says I’ve let the garden go, and I haven’t been keeping up with the walking group either. She says I’ve pushed almost everything else aside for this.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you actually felt unusually happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Happy? I don’t know if I’d call it happy exactly. There are moments I feel… lit up, I guess. Like I’ve got this purpose, like if I get the memorial just right, the whole town will finally understand who my brother was, who our family is.\n\nBut then it turns quick. If my wife says maybe the display is too much, or someone at the senior center tells me to slow down, I get sharp with them. I snap before I even think. So it’s not just happy—it’s more like I’m charged up, and people keep getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Since your brother died, have you had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… two weeks is hard to measure. Since he died, I’ve had low moments, sure. I miss him, and some mornings I sit there thinking about things we should’ve said.\n\nBut most of the time I’ve been busy with the memorial, and that sort of carries me along. My wife would probably tell you I don’t sit still long enough to be down. Though after my father passed, I remember my mother wore black for nearly a year—different times, you know, people didn’t talk about feelings the way they do now.\n\nSo… depressed most of the day, nearly every day? I’m not sure I’d say that. I’ve been upset, yes, but I don’t know about like that.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had more energy than usual nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I suppose I have. I don’t sit in my chair the way I used to, that’s for sure. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to sort one little box, and next thing I’m hauling things from the closet, stacking papers on the table, making three phone calls, walking back and forth from the kitchen to the den like I forgot what room I belong in.\n\nIt’s funny, my father used to say a man slows down after a certain age, but lately I feel like there’s more steam in me than there ought to be. Not young exactly, but like my body keeps pushing me up again every time I try to settle.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you been sleeping much less than usual and still felt rested?\n\nPatient: Sleep… I don’t know. I mean, at my age sleep gets funny anyway, doesn’t it? My mother used to wake up before dawn every day and say that was just how the house told you it was morning.\n\nI’ve had some nights where I’m up earlier, or I don’t stay asleep the way I used to, but whether I’m “rested”… that’s harder to say. Sometimes I get moving because there’s something to do, not because I woke up feeling like I had a full night. I haven’t really kept track of the hours.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have your thoughts been racing?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do jump around on me. I’ll start thinking, “I should make a proper display case,” you know, something nice, and before I’ve even finished that thought I’m thinking maybe I ought to drive out and find that cousin we lost touch with, and then some old ballad lyric pops in my head—my brother used to hum one, or maybe it was my uncle, I can’t remember.\n\nAnd then I try to write it all down so it makes sense, but it’s like the thoughts won’t line up. They keep cutting ahead of each other. I wouldn’t say I’m trying to think fast, it just… happens that way.\n\nDoctor: Has this memorial planning caused serious problems in your day-to-day life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, though I hate calling it “serious,” because that makes it sound like I’m doing something wrong.\n\nBut I’ve missed my walking group a few times, and the book club too. I used to like sitting there and hearing what everyone thought, even if half of them never finished the book. And the garden—oh, my tomatoes would be ashamed of me this year. I keep meaning to get out there, but then I see something that should go in the memorial display, or I think of someone I need to call, and the whole day turns.\n\nPeople at the senior center have gotten upset with me. They say I keep bringing everything back to the memorial, and maybe I do. My wife says our days are all about calming me down or stopping me from making another call, taking another trip, starting another argument. I don’t mean for it to be that way, but it’s like everything else gets pushed to the side.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you been more distractible than usual?\n\nPatient: Distractible… I’m not sure how to measure that. I’ve always been the sort who notices little things—the kettle making that high sound, a bird at the window, a bit of dust on a picture frame—and then I remember something my father used to say about keeping a house presentable.\n\nBut these past couple months… I don’t know. My wife might say I don’t stay on one thing, but sometimes I think that’s just because there are a lot of things needing attention. I’ll start to answer a letter, then see a clipping, then think I should put it somewhere safe. Is that distractible? I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you felt more self-confident than usual?\n\nPatient: More self-confident… I’m not sure I’d call it that. I mean, some days I feel very certain about what ought to be done, but confidence is a funny word, isn’t it? My father used to say confidence was just knowing how to stand straight in church even when your shoes hurt.\n\nI haven’t really thought, “Oh, I’m better than everyone,” if that’s what you mean. I just… I don’t know. I’ve been more set on certain things, maybe. But self-confident? I’d have to think about that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates":["F31.9","F43.23","F43.20"],"note":"","is_final":false}

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Bipolar I Disorder with Psychotic Features. You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S019\n  name: Delusions\n  description: Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence.\n  manifestation: I know it sounds odd, but I’m certain those old ballads my brother kept aren’t just songs—they’ve got warnings tucked in them for me, and when my wife says I’m reading too much into it, I still know what I heard in those words.\n- key: S020\n  name: Hallucinations\n  description: Perception-like experiences that occur without an external stimulus and are as vivid and clear as normal perception.\n  manifestation: Sometimes when I’m sorting his song sheets at the kitchen table, I hear my brother’s voice clear as day humming behind me or telling me which tune comes next, even though nobody else in the house hears a thing.\n- key: S033\n  name: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\n  description: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.\n  manifestation: I’ll feel lit up and full of purpose one minute, like the whole town is finally going to understand our family, and then I snap at my wife or someone at the senior center if they slow me down or question the display.\n- key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: I can’t seem to sit in my chair like I used to; I used to like sitting there and hearing what everyone thought, even if half of them never finished the book. And the garden—oh, my tomatoes would be ashamed of me this year. I keep meaning to get out there, but then I see something that should go in the memorial display, or I think of someone I need to call, and the whole day turns.\n\nPeople at the senior center have gotten upset with me. They say I keep bringing everything back to the memorial, and maybe I do. My wife says our days are all about calming me down or stopping me from making another call, taking another trip, starting another argument. I don’t mean for it to be that way, but it’s like everything else gets pushed to the side.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you been more distractible than usual?\n\nPatient: Distractible… I’m not sure how to measure that. I’ve always been the sort who notices little things—the kettle making that high sound, a bird at the window, a bit of dust on a picture frame—and then I remember something my father used to say about keeping a house presentable.\n\nBut these past couple months… I don’t know. My wife might say I don’t stay on one thing, but sometimes I think that’s just because there are a lot of things needing attention. I’ll start to answer a letter, then see a clipping, then think I should put it somewhere safe. Is that distractible? I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you felt more self-confident than usual?\n\nPatient: More self-confident… I’m not sure I’d call it that. I mean, some days I feel very certain about what ought to be done, but confidence is a funny word, isn’t it? My father used to say confidence was just knowing how to stand straight in church even when your shoes hurt.\n\nI haven’t really thought, “Oh, I’m better than everyone,” if that’s what you mean. I just… I don’t know. I’ve been more set on certain things, maybe. But self-confident? I’d have to think about that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring these past two months, have you felt unusually irritable most of the day nearly every day?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… irritable, I suppose that’s fair. Not all the time exactly, but it comes on strong. I’ll feel sort of lit up, like I’ve finally got the purpose of this memorial clear in my head and the whole town is going to understand what our family meant, what my father stood for—he had this way of polishing his shoes before every parade, you know, even if it rained.

And then if my wife says, “Maybe wait before calling them,” or someone at the senior center questions where the display should go, I snap. I don’t mean to. It’s just… they feel like they’re slowing down something important. Then afterward I know I sounded sharp. But in the moment, yes, I get very irritated.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF31.9, F43.23, F43.20\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess it’s this memorial I’m putting together for my brother. It started out simple, you know, just a few songs and maybe some pictures, but then I got to thinking about the old places we used to go, and who should be invited, and the displays, and now I’m on the phone most days tracking people down and planning trips and all that.\n\nMy wife says I’ve let the garden go, and I haven’t been showing up for the walking group much either. She says I’ve pushed everything else aside for it. I don’t know, it just feels important, but it’s gotten bigger than I meant it to.\n\nDoctor: While you’ve been working on the memorial, have you felt unusually upbeat or energized compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose I have. Some days I feel sort of lit up, like I’ve got this purpose, like if I get it all right then the whole town will finally understand what our family was about.\n\nBut then if my wife says I’m overdoing it, or someone at the senior center starts questioning where the display should go, I get sharp with them. I don’t mean to, but I snap, like they’re getting in the way of something important.\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “need” less sleep. I mean, some nights I’m up later because I start thinking about who still needs calling, or I remember a box of photos in the closet—funny, my brother had this old fishing picture everyone used to laugh about—and then I’m looking for it and the evening gets away from me.\n\nBut whether I’m actually sleeping less than usual… I’m not sure. I haven’t really been keeping track.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that you’ve been talking faster than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Yes, my pastor has said something like that. At the senior center meetings, I start explaining how the songs fit with the quilts and the photos, and then one thing reminds me of another—like the hymn my brother liked, though honestly he’d only admit it after a couple coffees—and before I know it, I’ve talked right over three people.\n\nMy pastor will sort of put his hand up gentle and say, “Aaron, let’s let someone else speak.” So… yes, I guess people have noticed I’m going on too fast or too much.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt that you have a special ability or unique responsibility to make this memorial matter more than anyone else could?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d put it like that… “special ability” sounds awfully grand. I just—well, I’ve been around long enough to remember how my mother set tables for half the neighborhood, and how my brother used to fix people’s porch steps without taking a dime, and those things get lost if somebody doesn’t put them in the right order.\n\nNow, whether that means I’m the only one who can do it… I don’t know. My wife says other people can help, and she’s probably right. It just feels like I know the pieces, you know? Like that picture from the church picnic—half the folks in it are gone now, and if I don’t say who they are, then who’s going to?\n\nDoctor: Have you been more active than usual in planning or doing things lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. That’s kind of what brought me in, really. This memorial for my brother started as a little thing, you know, maybe a few songs and some pictures, and now I’m spending most days chasing down hymns, calling people, figuring out displays, even thinking about trips to pick up things or talk to folks.\n\nAnd I mean, it all feels important—my brother was the kind of man who’d show up with a toolbox before you even asked—but my wife says I’ve let the garden go, and I haven’t been to the walking group much, and just about everything else has gotten pushed aside. So yes, I’ve been doing a lot more than usual, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this unusually energized and driven?\n\nPatient: About two months, I’d say. It really started after my brother passed and I began working on his tribute. At first it was just, you know, picking a song or two, maybe sorting some photos, but then I kept seeing more that ought to be included—people he helped, stories from church, little things like that.\n\nSo… yes, roughly two months I’ve been feeling this driven about it.\n\nDoctor: Before your brother died, had you ever had another period when you felt unusually energized and driven like this?\n\nPatient: Well… before he died? I’m trying to think. I’ve always been a person who gets involved in things—church suppers, fixing up the house, helping my wife with odds and ends—but this particular push, this kind of being pulled along by it, has really been these last couple months.\n\nIt started after my brother passed and I began putting together the tribute for him. Before that, I don’t know that I’d say it was like this. This has been going on about two months, tied up with the memorial.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you felt more important or more capable than usual?\n\nPatient: More important? I don’t know… I haven’t really thought of it that way. I mean, I do feel like this memorial needs to be done right, but that’s because he was my brother, you know? He helped a lot of people.\n\nCapable… well, I’ve been making calls and arranging things, but I wouldn’t say I suddenly feel like some big important man. Though my wife did say I was acting like I was running the whole church committee, and that reminds me, Mrs. Hanley used to run those bake sales like a general—she could get forty pies donated by Thursday without raising her voice.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I feel responsible for it, I guess, more than important.\n\nDoctor: Aside from working on your brother’s memorial, have you been taking on other new projects or activities more than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, not so much new projects besides the memorial, I guess. It’s more that the memorial itself keeps turning into ten different projects. I start with one song, then I’m calling someone about photos, then I’m planning a display, then I think maybe I should drive over and talk to somebody who knew him from church years back.\n\nMost days I’m wrapped up in that—songs, calls, displays, little trips—and my wife says I’ve let the garden go, and I haven’t been keeping up with the walking group either. She says I’ve pushed almost everything else aside for this.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you actually felt unusually happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Happy? I don’t know if I’d call it happy exactly. There are moments I feel… lit up, I guess. Like I’ve got this purpose, like if I get the memorial just right, the whole town will finally understand who my brother was, who our family is.\n\nBut then it turns quick. If my wife says maybe the display is too much, or someone at the senior center tells me to slow down, I get sharp with them. I snap before I even think. So it’s not just happy—it’s more like I’m charged up, and people keep getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Since your brother died, have you had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… two weeks is hard to measure. Since he died, I’ve had low moments, sure. I miss him, and some mornings I sit there thinking about things we should’ve said.\n\nBut most of the time I’ve been busy with the memorial, and that sort of carries me along. My wife would probably tell you I don’t sit still long enough to be down. Though after my father passed, I remember my mother wore black for nearly a year—different times, you know, people didn’t talk about feelings the way they do now.\n\nSo… depressed most of the day, nearly every day? I’m not sure I’d say that. I’ve been upset, yes, but I don’t know about like that.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had more energy than usual nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I suppose I have. I don’t sit in my chair the way I used to, that’s for sure. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to sort one little box, and next thing I’m hauling things from the closet, stacking papers on the table, making three phone calls, walking back and forth from the kitchen to the den like I forgot what room I belong in.\n\nIt’s funny, my father used to say a man slows down after a certain age, but lately I feel like there’s more steam in me than there ought to be. Not young exactly, but like my body keeps pushing me up again every time I try to settle.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you been sleeping much less than usual and still felt rested?\n\nPatient: Sleep… I don’t know. I mean, at my age sleep gets funny anyway, doesn’t it? My mother used to wake up before dawn every day and say that was just how the house told you it was morning.\n\nI’ve had some nights where I’m up earlier, or I don’t stay asleep the way I used to, but whether I’m “rested”… that’s harder to say. Sometimes I get moving because there’s something to do, not because I woke up feeling like I had a full night. I haven’t really kept track of the hours.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have your thoughts been racing?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do jump around on me. I’ll start thinking, “I should make a proper display case,” you know, something nice, and before I’ve even finished that thought I’m thinking maybe I ought to drive out and find that cousin we lost touch with, and then some old ballad lyric pops in my head—my brother used to hum one, or maybe it was my uncle, I can’t remember.\n\nAnd then I try to write it all down so it makes sense, but it’s like the thoughts won’t line up. They keep cutting ahead of each other. I wouldn’t say I’m trying to think fast, it just… happens that way.\n\nDoctor: Has this memorial planning caused serious problems in your day-to-day life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, though I hate calling it “serious,” because that makes it sound like I’m doing something wrong.\n\nBut I’ve missed my walking group a few times, and the book club too. I used to like sitting there and hearing what everyone thought, even if half of them never finished the book. And the garden—oh, my tomatoes would be ashamed of me this year. I keep meaning to get out there, but then I see something that should go in the memorial display, or I think of someone I need to call, and the whole day turns.\n\nPeople at the senior center have gotten upset with me. They say I keep bringing everything back to the memorial, and maybe I do. My wife says our days are all about calming me down or stopping me from making another call, taking another trip, starting another argument. I don’t mean for it to be that way, but it’s like everything else gets pushed to the side.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you been more distractible than usual?\n\nPatient: Distractible… I’m not sure how to measure that. I’ve always been the sort who notices little things—the kettle making that high sound, a bird at the window, a bit of dust on a picture frame—and then I remember something my father used to say about keeping a house presentable.\n\nBut these past couple months… I don’t know. My wife might say I don’t stay on one thing, but sometimes I think that’s just because there are a lot of things needing attention. I’ll start to answer a letter, then see a clipping, then think I should put it somewhere safe. Is that distractible? I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you felt more self-confident than usual?\n\nPatient: More self-confident… I’m not sure I’d call it that. I mean, some days I feel very certain about what ought to be done, but confidence is a funny word, isn’t it? My father used to say confidence was just knowing how to stand straight in church even when your shoes hurt.\n\nI haven’t really thought, “Oh, I’m better than everyone,” if that’s what you mean. I just… I don’t know. I’ve been more set on certain things, maybe. But self-confident? I’d have to think about that.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you felt unusually irritable most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… irritable, I suppose that’s fair. Not all the time exactly, but it comes on strong. I’ll feel sort of lit up, like I’ve finally got the purpose of this memorial clear in my head and the whole town is going to understand what our family meant, what my father stood for—he had this way of polishing his shoes before every parade, you know, even if it rained.\n\nAnd then if my wife says, “Maybe wait before calling them,” or someone at the senior center questions where the display should go, I snap. I don’t mean to. It’s just… they feel like they’re slowing down something important. Then afterward I know I sounded sharp. But in the moment, yes, I get very irritated.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like I’ve got this purpose, like if I get the memorial just right, the whole town will finally understand who my brother was, who our family is.\n\nBut then it turns quick. If my wife says maybe the display is too much, or someone at the senior center tells me to slow down, I get sharp with them. I snap before I even think. So it’s not just happy—it’s more like I’m charged up, and people keep getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Since your brother died, have you had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… two weeks is hard to measure. Since he died, I’ve had low moments, sure. I miss him, and some mornings I sit there thinking about things we should’ve said.\n\nBut most of the time I’ve been busy with the memorial, and that sort of carries me along. My wife would probably tell you I don’t sit still long enough to be down. Though after my father passed, I remember my mother wore black for nearly a year—different times, you know, people didn’t talk about feelings the way they do now.\n\nSo… depressed most of the day, nearly every day? I’m not sure I’d say that. I’ve been upset, yes, but I don’t know about like that.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had more energy than usual nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I suppose I have. I don’t sit in my chair the way I used to, that’s for sure. I’ll tell myself I’m just going to sort one little box, and next thing I’m hauling things from the closet, stacking papers on the table, making three phone calls, walking back and forth from the kitchen to the den like I forgot what room I belong in.\n\nIt’s funny, my father used to say a man slows down after a certain age, but lately I feel like there’s more steam in me than there ought to be. Not young exactly, but like my body keeps pushing me up again every time I try to settle.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you been sleeping much less than usual and still felt rested?\n\nPatient: Sleep… I don’t know. I mean, at my age sleep gets funny anyway, doesn’t it? My mother used to wake up before dawn every day and say that was just how the house told you it was morning.\n\nI’ve had some nights where I’m up earlier, or I don’t stay asleep the way I used to, but whether I’m “rested”… that’s harder to say. Sometimes I get moving because there’s something to do, not because I woke up feeling like I had a full night. I haven’t really kept track of the hours.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have your thoughts been racing?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do jump around on me. I’ll start thinking, “I should make a proper display case,” you know, something nice, and before I’ve even finished that thought I’m thinking maybe I ought to drive out and find that cousin we lost touch with, and then some old ballad lyric pops in my head—my brother used to hum one, or maybe it was my uncle, I can’t remember.\n\nAnd then I try to write it all down so it makes sense, but it’s like the thoughts won’t line up. They keep cutting ahead of each other. I wouldn’t say I’m trying to think fast, it just… happens that way.\n\nDoctor: Has this memorial planning caused serious problems in your day-to-day life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, though I hate calling it “serious,” because that makes it sound like I’m doing something wrong.\n\nBut I’ve missed my walking group a few times, and the book club too. I used to like sitting there and hearing what everyone thought, even if half of them never finished the book. And the garden—oh, my tomatoes would be ashamed of me this year. I keep meaning to get out there, but then I see something that should go in the memorial display, or I think of someone I need to call, and the whole day turns.\n\nPeople at the senior center have gotten upset with me. They say I keep bringing everything back to the memorial, and maybe I do. My wife says our days are all about calming me down or stopping me from making another call, taking another trip, starting another argument. I don’t mean for it to be that way, but it’s like everything else gets pushed to the side.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you been more distractible than usual?\n\nPatient: Distractible… I’m not sure how to measure that. I’ve always been the sort who notices little things—the kettle making that high sound, a bird at the window, a bit of dust on a picture frame—and then I remember something my father used to say about keeping a house presentable.\n\nBut these past couple months… I don’t know. My wife might say I don’t stay on one thing, but sometimes I think that’s just because there are a lot of things needing attention. I’ll start to answer a letter, then see a clipping, then think I should put it somewhere safe. Is that distractible? I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you felt more self-confident than usual?\n\nPatient: More self-confident… I’m not sure I’d call it that. My father used to say confidence was just knowing how to stand straight in church even when your shoes hurt.\n\nI haven’t really thought, “Oh, I’m better than everyone,” if that’s what you mean. I just… I don’t know. I’ve been more set on certain things, maybe. But self-confident? I’d have to think about that.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you felt unusually irritable most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… irritable, I suppose that’s fair. I don’t mean to. It’s just… they feel like they’re slowing down something important. Then afterward I know I sounded sharp. Though my wife did say I was acting like I was running the whole church committee, and that reminds me, Mrs. Hanley used to run those bake sales like a general—she could get forty pies donated by Thursday without raising her voice.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. She says I’ve pushed almost everything else aside for this.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you actually felt unusually happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Happy? I don’t know if I’d call it happy exactly. There are moments I feel… lit up, I guess. Like I’ve got this purpose, like if I get the memorial just right, the whole town will finally understand who my brother was, who our family is.\n\nBut then it turns quick. If my wife says maybe the display is too much, or someone at the senior center tells me to slow down, I get sharp with them. I snap before I even think. So it’s not just happy—it’s more like I’m charged up, and people keep getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Since your brother died, have you had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… two weeks is hard to measure. Since he died, I’ve had low moments, sure. I miss him, and some mornings I sit there thinking about things we should’ve said.\n\nBut most of the time I’ve been busy with the memorial, and that sort of carries me along. I’m not sure I’d say that. I’ve been upset, yes, but I don’t know about like that.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you had more energy than usual nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I suppose I have. I don’t sit in my chair the way I used to, that’s for sure. Not young exactly, but like my body keeps pushing me up again every time I try to settle.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you been sleeping much less than usual and still felt rested?\n\nPatient: Sleep… I don’t know. I mean, at my age sleep gets funny anyway, doesn’t it? My mother used to wake up before dawn every day and say that was just how the house told you it was morning.\n\nI’ve had some nights where I’m up earlier, or I don’t stay asleep the way I used to, but whether I’m “rested”… that’s harder to say. Sometimes I get moving because there’s something to do, not because I woke up feeling like I had a full night. I haven’t really kept track of the hours.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have your thoughts been racing?\n\nPatient: Yes… they do jump around on me. I’ll start thinking, “I should make a proper display case,” you know, something nice, and before I’ve even finished that thought I’m thinking maybe I ought to drive out and find that cousin we lost touch with, and then some old ballad lyric pops in my head—my brother used to hum one, or maybe it was my uncle, I can’t remember.\n\nAnd then I try to write it all down so it makes sense, but it’s like the thoughts won’t line up. They keep cutting ahead of each other. I wouldn’t say I’m trying to think fast, it just… happens that way.\n\nDoctor: Has this memorial planning caused serious problems in your day-to-day life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, though I hate calling it “serious,” because that makes it sound like I’m doing something wrong.\n\nBut I’ve missed my walking group a few times, and the book club too. I used to like sitting there and hearing what everyone thought, even if half of them never finished the book. And the garden—oh, my tomatoes would be ashamed of me this year. I keep meaning to get out there, but then I see something that should go in the memorial display, or I think of someone I need to call, and the whole day turns.\n\nPeople at the senior center have gotten upset with me. They say I keep bringing everything back to the memorial, and maybe I do. My wife says our days are all about calming me down or stopping me from making another call, taking another trip, starting another argument. I don’t mean for it to be that way, but it’s like everything else gets pushed to the side.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you been more distractible than usual?\n\nPatient: Distractible… I’m not sure how to measure that. I’ve always been the sort who notices little things—the kettle making that high sound, a bird at the window, a bit of dust on a picture frame—and then I remember something my father used to say about keeping a house presentable.\n\nBut these past couple months… I don’t know. My wife might say I don’t stay on one thing, but sometimes I think that’s just because there are a lot of things needing attention. I’ll start to answer a letter, then see a clipping, then think I should put it somewhere safe. Is that distractible? I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you felt more self-confident than usual?\n\nPatient: More self-confident… I’m not sure I’d call it that. I mean, some days I feel very certain about what ought to be done, but confidence is a funny word, isn’t it? My father used to say confidence was just knowing how to stand straight in church even when your shoes hurt.\n\nI haven’t really thought, “Oh, I’m better than everyone,” if that’s what you mean. I just… I don’t know. I’ve been more set on certain things, maybe. But self-confident? I’d have to think about that.\n\nDoctor: During these past two months, have you felt unusually irritable most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… irritable, I suppose that’s fair. I don’t mean to. It’s just… they feel like they’re slowing down something important. Then afterward I know I sounded sharp.
